In 1931 Himmler organised an SS intelligence service which became known as the *[Sicherheitsdienst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sicherheitsdienst)* (SD; Security Service) under his deputy, Heydrich.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012125-253] This organisation was tasked with locating and arresting communists and other political opponents.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012212–213-254][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeale2012411-255] Himmler established the beginnings of a parallel economy under the auspices of the SS Economy and Administration Head Office. This holding company owned housing corporations, factories, and publishing houses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESereny1996323,_329-256][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008343-257]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Nazi_Germany)

### Reich economics



[IG Farben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IG_Farben) synthetic oil plant under construction at [Buna Werke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monowitz_Buna_Werke) (1941). This plant was part of the complex at [Auschwitz concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp).

The most pressing economic matter the Nazis initially faced was the 30 per cent national unemployment rate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeLong1997-258] The economist [Hjalmar Schacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hjalmar_Schacht), President of the [Reichsbank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichsbank) and Minister of Economics, created a scheme for deficit financing in May 1933. Capital projects were paid for with the issuance of promissory notes called [Mefo bills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mefo_bills). When the notes were presented for payment, the Reichsbank printed money. Hitler and his economic team expected that the upcoming territorial expansion would provide the means of repaying the soaring national debt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005345-259] Schacht's administration achieved a rapid decline in the unemployment rate, the largest of any country during the Great Depression.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeLong1997-258] Economic recovery was uneven, with reduced hours of work and erratic availability of necessities, leading to disenchantment with the regime as early as 1934.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze200697-260]

In October 1933 the [Junkers Aircraft Works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junkers) was expropriated. In concert with other aircraft manufacturers and under the direction of Aviation Minister Göring, production was ramped up. From a workforce of 3,200 people producing 100 units per year in 1932, the industry grew to employ a quarter of a million workers manufacturing over 10,000 technically advanced aircraft annually less than ten years later.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze2006125–127-261]

An elaborate bureaucracy was created to regulate imports of raw materials and finished goods with the intention of eliminating foreign competition in the German marketplace and improving the nation's [balance of payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balance_of_payments). The Nazis encouraged the development of synthetic replacements for materials such as oil and textiles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze2006131-262] As the market was experiencing a glut and prices for petroleum were low, in 1933 the Nazi government made a profit-sharing agreement with [IG Farben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IG_Farben), guaranteeing them a 5 per cent return on capital invested in their synthetic oil plant at [Leuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leuna). Any profits in excess of that amount would be turned over to the Reich. By 1936, Farben regretted making the deal, as excess profits were by then being generated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze2006106,_117–118-263] In another attempt to secure an adequate wartime supply of petroleum, Germany intimidated [Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania) into signing a trade agreement in March 1939.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze2006308–309-264]

Major public works projects financed with deficit spending included the construction of a network of *[Autobahnen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichsautobahn)* and providing funding for programmes initiated by the previous government for housing and agricultural improvements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005322–326,_329-265] To stimulate the construction industry, credit was offered to private businesses and subsidies were made available for home purchases and repairs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005320-266] On the condition that the wife would leave the workforce, a loan of up to 1,000 Reichsmarks could be accessed by young couples of Aryan descent who intended to marry, and the amount that had to be repaid was reduced by 25 per cent for each child born.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005330–331-267] The caveat that the woman had to remain unemployed outside the home was dropped by 1937 due to a shortage of skilled labourers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005166-268]

Envisioning widespread car ownership as part of the new Germany, Hitler arranged for designer [Ferdinand Porsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Porsche) to draw up plans for the *KdF-wagen* ([Strength Through Joy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strength_Through_Joy) car), intended to be an automobile that everyone could afford. A prototype was displayed at the [International Motor Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Motor_Show_Germany) in Berlin on 17 February 1939. With the outbreak of World War II, the factory was converted to produce military vehicles. None were sold until after the war, when the vehicle was renamed the [Volkswagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_Beetle) (people's car).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005327–328,_338-269]



(from left) Hitler; [Robert Ley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Ley), head of the [German Labour Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Labour_Front); [Ferdinand Porsche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Porsche), armaments manufacturer; and [Hermann Göring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_G%C3%B6ring), head of the [Four Year Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Year_Plan) (1942)

Six million people were unemployed when the Nazis took power in 1933 and by 1937 there were fewer than a million.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005328,_333-270] This was in part due to the removal of women from the workforce.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005331-271] Real wages dropped by 25 per cent between 1933 and 1938.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeLong1997-258] After the dissolution of the trade unions in May 1933, their funds were seized and their leadership arrested,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008289-272] including those who attempted to co-operate with the Nazis.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960202-44] A new organisation, the [German Labour Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Labour_Front), was created and placed under the Nazi Party functionary [Robert Ley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Ley).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008289-272] Many unemployed people were forcibly drafted into this organisation, where they were given uniforms and tools and put to work. As a result, unemployed people disappeared from the streets, contributing to the perception that the Nazis were improving economic conditions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChilders2001-273] The average work week was 43 hours in 1933; by 1939 this increased to 47 hours.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcNab200954,_71-274]

By early 1934, the focus shifted towards rearmament. By 1935, military expenditures accounted for 73 per cent of the government's purchases of goods and services.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze200661–62-275] On 18 October 1936, Hitler named Göring as [Plenipotentiary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plenipotentiary) of the Four Year Plan, intended to speed up rearmament.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005357–360-276] In addition to calling for the rapid construction of steel mills, synthetic rubber plants, and other factories, Göring instituted [wage and price controls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wage_and_price_controls) and restricted the issuance of [stock dividends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stock_dividend).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeLong1997-258] Large expenditures were made on rearmament in spite of growing deficits.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005360-277] Plans unveiled in late 1938 for massive increases to the navy and air force were impossible to fulfil, as Germany lacked the finances and material resources to build the planned units, as well as the necessary fuel required to keep them running.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze2006294-278] With the introduction of compulsory military service in 1935, the *Reichswehr*, which had been limited to 100,000 by the terms of the Versailles Treaty, expanded to 750,000 on active service at the start of World War II, with a million more in the reserve.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005141–142-279] By January 1939, unemployment was down to 301,800 and it dropped to only 77,500 by September.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcNab200959-280]

### Wartime economy and forced labour

Further information: [Forced labour under German rule during World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labour_under_German_rule_during_World_War_II)

See also: [List of companies involved in the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_companies_involved_in_the_Holocaust)



Woman with *[Ostarbeiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostarbeiter)* badge at the [IG Farben](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IG_Farben) plant in Auschwitz

The Nazi war economy was a [mixed economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_economy) that combined a free market with central planning. The historian [Richard Overy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Overy) describes it as being somewhere in between the [command economy of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union) and the [capitalist system of the United States](32022.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOvery2006252-281]

In 1942, after the death of Armaments Minister [Fritz Todt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fritz_Todt), Hitler appointed Albert Speer as his replacement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeer1971263–264-282] Wartime rationing of consumer goods led to an increase in personal savings, funds which were in turn lent to the government to support the war effort.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETooze2006354–356-283] By 1944, the war was consuming 75 per cent of Germany's [gross domestic product](12594.md), compared to 60 per cent in the Soviet Union and 55 per cent in Britain.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008333-284] Speer improved production by centralising planning and control, reducing production of consumer goods, and [using forced labour and slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forced_labour_under_German_rule_during_World_War_II).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeer1971337-285][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFest1999142–144,_146–150-286] The wartime economy eventually relied heavily upon the large-scale employment of [slave labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slave_labour). Germany imported and enslaved some 12 million people from 20 European countries to work in factories and on farms. Approximately 75 per cent were Eastern European.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBeyer_&_Schneider-287] Many were casualties of Allied bombing, as they received poor air raid protection. Poor living conditions led to high rates of sickness, injury, and death, as well as sabotage and criminal activity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPanayi2005490,_495-288] The wartime economy also relied upon large-scale robbery, initially through the state seizing the property of Jewish citizens and later by plundering the resources of occupied territories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHamblet2008267–268-289]

[Foreign workers](384142.md) brought into Germany were put into four classifications: guest workers, military internees, civilian workers, and Eastern workers. Each group was subject to different regulations. The Nazis issued a ban on sexual relations between Germans and foreign workers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENazi_forced_labour1942-290][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpecial_treatment1942-291]

By 1944 over a half million women served as auxiliaries in the German armed forces.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSHMM,_''Women_in_the_Third_Reich''-292] The number of women in paid employment only increased by 271,000 (1.8 per cent) from 1939 to 1944.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008361-293] As the production of consumer goods had been cut back, women left those industries for employment in the war economy. They also took jobs formerly held by men, especially on farms and in family-owned shops.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008358–359-294]

Very heavy [strategic bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strategic_bombing_during_World_War_II) by the Allies [targeted refineries producing synthetic oil and gasoline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_Campaign_of_World_War_II), as well as the German transportation system, especially rail yards and canals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis1995-295] The armaments industry began to break down by September 1944. By November, fuel coal was no longer reaching its destinations and the production of new armaments was no longer possible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeer1971524–527-296] Overy argues that the bombing strained the German war economy and forced it to divert up to one-fourth of its manpower and industry into anti-aircraft resources, which very likely shortened the war.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOvery2006128–130-297]

## Racial policy and eugenics

Main articles: [Racial policy of Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_policy_of_Nazi_Germany) and [Nazi eugenics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_eugenics)

### Racism and antisemitism

Main article: [Nazi racial theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_racial_theories)

Racism and [antisemitism](1078.md) were basic tenets of the Nazi Party and the Nazi regime. Nazi Germany's racial policy was based on their belief in the existence of a superior [master race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Master_race). The Nazis postulated the existence of a racial conflict between the [Aryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aryan_race) master race and inferior races, particularly Jews, who were viewed as a mixed race that had infiltrated society and were responsible for the exploitation and repression of the Aryan race.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich201030–32-298]

### Persecution of Jews

Further information: [Anti-Jewish legislation in pre-war Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Jewish_legislation_in_pre-war_Nazi_Germany)



[Nazi boycott of Jewish businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_boycott_of_Jewish_businesses), April 1933. The posters say "Germans! Defend yourselves! Don't buy from Jews!"

Discrimination against Jews began immediately after the seizure of power. Following a month-long series of attacks by members of the SA on Jewish businesses and synagogues, on 1 April 1933 Hitler declared a [national boycott of Jewish businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_boycott_of_Jewish_businesses).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960203-299] The [Law for the Restoration of the Professional Civil Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_for_the_Restoration_of_the_Professional_Civil_Service) passed on 7 April forced all non-Aryan civil servants to retire from the legal profession and civil service.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajer200392-300] Similar legislation soon deprived other Jewish professionals of their right to practise, and on 11 April a decree was promulgated that stated anyone who had even one Jewish parent or grandparent was considered non-Aryan.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMajer200360-301] As part of the drive to remove Jewish influence from cultural life, members of the [National Socialist German Students' League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_German_Students'_League) removed from libraries any books considered un-German, and a nationwide [book burning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_book_burnings) was held on 10 May.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich201038–39-302]

The regime used violence and economic pressure to encourage Jews to leave the country voluntarily.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich201067–69-303] Jewish businesses were denied access to markets, forbidden to advertise, and deprived of access to government contracts. Citizens were harassed and subjected to violent attacks.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich201041-304] Many towns posted signs forbidding entry to Jews.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960233-305]

On 7 November 1938 a young Jewish man, [Herschel Grynszpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herschel_Grynszpan), shot and killed [Ernst vom Rath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_vom_Rath), a legation secretary at the German embassy in Paris, to protest against his family's treatment in Germany. This incident provided the pretext for a [pogrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pogrom) the Nazis incited against the Jews two days later. Members of the SA damaged or destroyed synagogues and Jewish property throughout Germany. At least 91 German Jews were murdered during this pogrom, later called *[Kristallnacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristallnacht)*, the Night of Broken Glass.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitchen2006273-306][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010112–113-307] Further restrictions were imposed on Jews in the coming months – they were forbidden to own businesses or work in retail shops, drive cars, go to the cinema, visit the library, or own weapons, and Jewish pupils were removed from schools. The Jewish community was fined one billion marks to pay for the damage caused by *Kristallnacht* and told that any insurance settlements would be confiscated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010117-308] By 1939 around 250,000 of Germany's 437,000 Jews had emigrated to the United States, Argentina, the United Kingdom, Palestine, and other countries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010127-309][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005555–558-310] Many chose to stay in continental Europe. Emigrants to Palestine were allowed to transfer property there under the terms of the [Haavara Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haavara_Agreement), but those moving to other countries had to leave virtually all their property behind, and it was seized by the government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005555–558-310]

### Persecution of Romani

Main article: [Romani Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_Holocaust)

Like the Jews, the [Romani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people) were subjected to persecution from the early days of the regime. The Romani were forbidden to marry people of German extraction. They were shipped to concentration camps starting in 1935 and many were murdered.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich201049-191][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008759-192] Following the invasion of Poland, 2,500 Roma and [Sinti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinti) people were deported from Germany to the General Government, where they were imprisoned in labour camps. The survivors were likely exterminated at [Bełżec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Be%C5%82%C5%BCec_extermination_camp), [Sobibor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sobibor_extermination_camp), or [Treblinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treblinka). A further 5,000 Sinti and Austrian Lalleri people were deported to the [Łódź Ghetto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C5%81%C3%B3d%C5%BA_Ghetto) in late 1941, where half were estimated to have died. The Romani survivors of the ghetto were subsequently moved to the [Chełmno extermination camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che%C5%82mno_extermination_camp) in early 1942.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSHMM,_''Genocide_of_European_Roma''-311]

The Nazis intended on deporting all Romani people from Germany, and confined them to *[Zigeunerlager](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zigeunerlager)* (Gypsy camps) for this purpose. Himmler ordered their deportation from Germany in December 1942, with few exceptions. A total of 23,000 Romani were deported to [Auschwitz concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp), of whom 19,000 died. Outside of Germany, the Romani people were regularly used for forced labour, though many were murdered outright. In the Baltic states and the Soviet Union, 30,000 Romani were murdered by the SS, the German Army, and *Einsatzgruppen*. In [occupied Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_the_Military_Commander_in_Serbia), 1,000 to 12,000 Romani were murdered, while nearly all 25,000 Romani living in the [Independent State of Croatia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_State_of_Croatia) were murdered. The estimates at end of the war put the total number of Romani victims at around 220,000, which equalled approximately 25 per cent of the Romani population in Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSHMM,_''Genocide_of_European_Roma''-311]

### Other persecuted groups

Main article: [Aktion T4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aktion_T4)



Poster from the Nazi Party's [Office of Racial Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Racial_Policy_\(Nazi_Party\)): "60 000 RM is what this person with hereditary illness costs the community in his lifetime. Fellow citizen, that is your money too."

Action T4 was a programme of systematic murder of the physically and mentally handicapped and patients in psychiatric hospitals that took place mainly from 1939 to 1941, and continued until the end of the war. Initially the victims were shot by the *Einsatzgruppen* and others; [gas chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_chamber) and [gas vans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_gas_van) using [carbon monoxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_monoxide) were used by early 1940.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010138–141-312][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans200875–76-313] Under the [Law for the Prevention of Hereditarily Diseased Offspring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_for_the_Prevention_of_Hereditarily_Diseased_Offspring), enacted on 14 July 1933, over 400,000 individuals underwent [compulsory sterilisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compulsory_sterilisation).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008295-314] Over half were those considered mentally deficient, which included not only people who scored poorly on intelligence tests, but those who deviated from expected standards of behaviour regarding thrift, sexual behaviour, and cleanliness. Most of the victims came from disadvantaged groups such as prostitutes, the poor, the homeless, and criminals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich201047–48-315] Other groups persecuted and murdered included Jehovah's Witnesses, homosexuals, social misfits, and [members of the political and religious opposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust#The_political_left).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008759-192][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENiewykNicosia200045-316]

### Generalplan Ost

Main article: [Generalplan Ost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalplan_Ost)

See also: [Hunger Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger_Plan)

[Germany's war in the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)) was based on Hitler's long-standing view that Jews were the great enemy of the German people and that *Lebensraum* was needed for Germany's expansion. Hitler focused his attention on Eastern Europe, aiming to conquer Poland and the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBendersky2007161-188][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGellately1996270–274-189] Hitler's belief in the racial inferiority of [Russians](59510.md), as well as [Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs) in general, had convinced him that a German conquest of Russia was inevitable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWeikart200974-317] After the occupation of Poland in 1939, all Jews living in the General Government were confined to [ghettos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghettos_in_Nazi-occupied_Europe), and those who were physically fit were required to perform compulsory labour.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2000a111-318] In 1941 Hitler decided to destroy the Polish nation completely; within 15 to 20 years the General Government was to be [cleared of ethnic Poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Poles_by_Nazi_Germany) and resettled by German colonists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerghahn199932-319] About 3.8 to 4 million Poles would remain as slaves,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowszechna_PWN2004267-320] part of a slave labour force of 14 million the Nazis intended to create using citizens of conquered nations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGellately1996270–274-189][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeinemann_et_al.2006-321]

To determine who should be killed, Himmler created the *[Volksliste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volksliste)*, a system of classification of people deemed to be of German blood.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOvery2005544-322] He ordered that those of Germanic descent who refused to be classified as ethnic Germans should be deported to concentration camps, have their children taken away, or be assigned to forced labour.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENicholas2006247-323][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELukas2001113-324] The plan also included the [kidnapping of children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kidnapping_of_children_for_forced_Germanization_by_Nazi_Germany) deemed to have Aryan-[Nordic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_race) traits, who were presumed to be of German descent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESereny1999-325] The goal was to implement *Generalplan Ost* after the conquest of the Soviet Union, but when the invasion failed Hitler had to consider other options.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder2010416-326][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008683-327] One suggestion was a mass forced deportation of Jews to Poland, Palestine, or Madagascar.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2000a111-318]

In addition, the Nazis planned to reduce the population of the conquered territories by 30 million people through starvation in an action called the [Hunger Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger_Plan). Food supplies would be diverted to the German army and German civilians. Cities would be razed and the land allowed to return to forest or resettled by German colonists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESnyder2010162–163,_416-328] Together, the Hunger Plan and *Generalplan Ost* would have led to the starvation of 80 million people in the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDorland20096-329] These partially fulfilled plans resulted in the [democidal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democide) deaths of an estimated 19.3 million civilians and [prisoners of war](25008.md) (POWs) throughout the USSR and elsewhere in Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERummel1994\[httpwwwhawaiiedupowerkillsNAZISTAB11GIF_table,_p._112\]-330] During the course of the war, the [Soviet Union lost a total of 27 million people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union); less than nine million of these were combat deaths.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHosking2006242-331] One in four of the Soviet population were killed or wounded.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1994204-332]

### The Holocaust and Final Solution

Main articles: [The Holocaust in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust_in_Germany) and [Final Solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Solution)

Further information: [The Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust)



A wagon piled high with corpses outside the crematorium in the [Buchenwald concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buchenwald_concentration_camp) liberated by the [U.S. Army](32087.md), 1945

Around the time of the failed offensive against Moscow in December 1941, [Hitler resolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Chancellery_meeting_of_12_December_1941) that the Jews of Europe were to be exterminated immediately.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich,_Chapter_172003-333] While the murder of Jewish civilians had been ongoing in the occupied territories of Poland and the Soviet Union, plans for the total eradication of the Jewish population of Europe—eleven million people—were formalised at the [Wannsee Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wannsee_Conference) on 20 January 1942. Some would be [worked to death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extermination_through_labour) and the rest would be murdered in the implementation of the [Final Solution to the Jewish Question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_Solution_to_the_Jewish_Question).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012555–556-334] Initially the victims were murdered by *Einsatzgruppen* firing squads, then by [stationary gas chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aktion_T4) or by [gas vans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_vans), but these methods proved impractical for an operation of this scale.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008256–257-335][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrowning2005188–190-336] By 1942 extermination camps equipped with gas chambers were established at [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_II), [Chełmno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che%C5%82mno_extermination_camp), Sobibor, Treblinka, and elsewhere.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2010279–280-337] The total number of Jews murdered is estimated at 5.5 to six million,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008318-251] including over a million children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSHMM,_''Children_during_the_Holocaust''-338]

The Allies received information about the murders from the [Polish government-in-exile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_government-in-exile) and Polish leadership in Warsaw, based mostly on intelligence from the [Polish underground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_underground).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFleming201431–32,_35–36-339][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008559–560-340] German citizens had access to information about what was happening, as soldiers returning from the occupied territories reported on what they had seen and done.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008555–556,_560-341] Historian [Richard J. Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_J._Evans) states that most German citizens disapproved of the genocide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008560–561-342][^cite_note-343]

### Oppression of ethnic Poles

Main article: [Genocide of Poles by Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genocide_of_Poles_by_Nazi_Germany)

Further information: [Occupation of Poland (1939–1945)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Poland_\(1939%E2%80%931945\))

Poles were viewed by Nazis as subhuman non-Aryans, and during the German [occupation of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Poland_\(1939%E2%80%9345\)) 2.7 million ethnic Poles died.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaterskiSzarota20099-344] Polish civilians were subject to forced labour in German industry, [internment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internment), [wholesale expulsions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expulsion_of_Poles_by_Nazi_Germany) to make way for German colonists, and mass executions. The German authorities engaged in a systematic [effort to destroy Polish culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_culture_during_World_War_II) and national identity. During operation [AB-Aktion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AB-Aktion), many university professors and members of the Polish intelligentsia were arrested, transported to concentration camps, or executed. During the war, Poland lost an estimated 39 to 45 per cent of its physicians and dentists, 26 to 57 per cent of its lawyers, 15 to 30 per cent of its teachers, 30 to 40 per cent of its scientists and university professors, and 18 to 28 per cent of its clergy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWrobel1999-345]

### Mistreatment of Soviet POWs

Main article: [German atrocities committed against Soviet prisoners of war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_atrocities_committed_against_Soviet_prisoners_of_war)



Soviet prisoners of war in [Mauthausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauthausen-Gusen_concentration_camp)

The Nazis captured 5.75 million Soviet prisoners of war, more than they took from all the other Allied powers combined. Of these, they killed an estimated 3.3 million,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960952-346] with 2.8 million of them being killed between June 1941 and January 1942.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldhagen1996290-347] Many POWs starved to death or resorted to cannibalism while being held in open-air pens at Auschwitz and elsewhere.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008295–296-348]

From 1942 onward, Soviet POWs were viewed as a source of forced labour, and received better treatment so they could work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960954-349] By December 1944, 750,000 Soviet POWs were working, including in German armaments factories (in violation of the [Hague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hague_Conventions_of_1899_and_1907) and [Geneva conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_conventions)), mines, and farms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960951,_954-350]

## Society

### Education

Further information: [University education in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_education_in_Nazi_Germany)

Antisemitic legislation passed in 1933 led to the removal of all Jewish teachers, professors, and officials from the education system. Most teachers were required to belong to the *[Nationalsozialistischer Lehrerbund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Teachers_League)* (NSLB; National Socialist Teachers League) and university professors were required to join the [National Socialist German Lecturers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_German_Lecturers_League).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENakosteen1965386-351][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPine201114–15,_27-352] Teachers had to take an oath of loyalty and obedience to Hitler, and those who failed to show sufficient conformity to party ideals were often reported by students or fellow teachers and dismissed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960249-353][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005270-354] Lack of funding for salaries led to many teachers leaving the profession. The average class size increased from 37 in 1927 to 43 in 1938 due to the resulting teacher shortage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005269-355]

Frequent and often contradictory directives were issued by Interior Minister Wilhelm Frick, [Bernhard Rust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernhard_Rust) of the [Reich Ministry of Science, Education and Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Ministry_of_Science,_Education_and_Culture), and other agencies regarding content of lessons and acceptable textbooks for use in primary and secondary schools.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005263–264,_270-356] Books deemed unacceptable to the regime were removed from school libraries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005264-357] Indoctrination in Nazi ideology was made compulsory in January 1934.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005264-357] Students selected as future members of the party elite were indoctrinated from the age of 12 at [Adolf Hitler Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler_Schools) for primary education and [National Political Institutes of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Political_Institutes_of_Education) for secondary education. Detailed indoctrination of future holders of elite military rank was undertaken at [Order Castles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NS-Ordensburgen).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960255-358]



The Nazi salute in school (1934): children were indoctrinated at an early age.

Primary and secondary education focused on racial biology, population policy, culture, geography, and physical fitness.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPine201113–40-359] The curriculum in most subjects, including biology, geography, and even arithmetic, was altered to change the focus to race.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005263–265-360] Military education became the central component of physical education, and education in physics was oriented toward subjects with military applications, such as ballistics and aerodynamics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarago197265-361][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005265-362] Students were required to watch all films prepared by the school division of the [Reich Ministry of Public Enlightenment and Propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Ministry_of_Public_Enlightenment_and_Propaganda).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005264-357]

At universities, appointments to top posts were the subject of power struggles between the education ministry, the university boards, and the National Socialist German Students' League.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005292-363] In spite of pressure from the League and various government ministries, most university professors did not make changes to their lectures or syllabus during the Nazi period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005302–303-364] This was especially true of universities located in predominantly Catholic regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005305-365] Enrolment at German universities declined from 104,000 students in 1931 to 41,000 in 1939, but enrolment in medical schools rose sharply as Jewish doctors had been forced to leave the profession, so medical graduates had good job prospects.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005295–297-366] From 1934, university students were required to attend frequent and time-consuming military training sessions run by the SA.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005293-367] First-year students also had to serve six months in a labour camp for the [Reich Labour Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Labour_Service); an additional ten weeks service were required of second-year students.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005299-368]

### Role of women and family

Main article: [Women in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_Nazi_Germany)

Women were a cornerstone of Nazi social policy. The Nazis opposed the feminist movement, claiming that it was the creation of Jewish intellectuals, instead advocating a [patriarchal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchal) society in which the German woman would recognise that her "world is her husband, her family, her children, and her home".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005331-271] Feminist groups were shut down or incorporated into the [National Socialist Women's League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialist_Women's_League), which coordinated groups throughout the country to promote motherhood and household activities. Courses were offered on childrearing, sewing, and cooking. Prominent feminists, including [Anita Augspurg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anita_Augspurg), [Lida Gustava Heymann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lida_Gustava_Heymann), and [Helene Stöcker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helene_St%C3%B6cker), felt forced to live in exile.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005516–517-369] The League published the *[NS-Frauen-Warte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NS-Frauen-Warte)*, the only Nazi-approved women's magazine in Nazi Germany;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeidelberg_University_Library-370] despite some propaganda aspects, it was predominantly an ordinary woman's magazine.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERupp197845-371]

Women were encouraged to leave the workforce, and the creation of large families by racially suitable women was promoted through propaganda campaigns. Women received a bronze award—known as the *[Ehrenkreuz der Deutschen Mutter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross_of_Honour_of_the_German_Mother)* (Cross of Honour of the German Mother)—for giving birth to four children, silver for six, and gold for eight or more.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005516–517-369] Large families received subsidies to help with expenses. Though the measures led to increases in the birth rate, the number of families having four or more children declined by five per cent between 1935 and 1940.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005518–519-372] Removing women from the workforce did not have the intended effect of freeing up jobs for men, as women were for the most part employed as domestic servants, weavers, or in the food and drink industries—jobs that were not of interest to men.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005332–333-373] Nazi philosophy prevented large numbers of women from being hired to work in munitions factories in the build-up to the war, so foreign labourers were brought in. After the war started, slave labourers were extensively used.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005369-374] In January 1943, Hitler signed a decree requiring all women under the age of fifty to report for work assignments to help the war effort.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008749-375] Thereafter women were funnelled into agricultural and industrial jobs, and by September 1944 14.9 million women were working in munitions production.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcNab2009164-376]

Nazi leaders endorsed the idea that rational and theoretical work was alien to a woman's nature, and as such discouraged women from seeking higher education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStephenson200170-377] A law passed in April 1933 limited the number of women admitted to university to ten per cent of the number of men.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005297-378] This resulted in female enrolment in secondary schools dropping from 437,000 in 1926 to 205,000 in 1937. The number of women enrolled in post-secondary schools dropped from 128,000 in 1933 to 51,000 in 1938. However, with the requirement that men be enlisted into the armed forces during the war, women comprised half of the enrolment in the post-secondary system by 1944.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPauley2003119–137-379]



Young women of the *[Bund Deutscher Mädel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_German_Girls)* (League of German Girls) practising gymnastics in 1941

Women were expected to be strong, healthy, vital,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOvery2005248-380] feminine, and submissive.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELee2016203–204-381] The sturdy peasant woman who [worked the land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_and_soil) and bore strong children was considered the ideal, and women were praised for being athletic and tanned from working outdoors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERupp197845–46-382] Organisations were created for the indoctrination of Nazi values. From 25 March 1939 membership in the [Hitler Youth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler_Youth) was made compulsory for all children over the age of ten.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005272-383] The *[Jungmädelbund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jungm%C3%A4delbund)* (Young Girls League) section of the Hitler Youth was for girls age 10 to 14, and the [*Bund Deutscher Mädel*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_German_Girls) (BDM; League of German Girls) for young women age 14 to 18. The BDM's activities focused on physical education.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrunberger1971278-384]

Pamphlets enjoined German women to avoid sexual relations with foreign workers as a danger to their blood,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERupp1978124–125-385] and sexual relations between foreign men and German women were heavily punished. In the case of prisoners of war, the death sentence could be issued.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScheck202161–63-386]

The regime supported a conservative code of conduct in sexual matters, and regarded sexual liberation as a Jewish phenomenon. Homosexuals and others who were sexually different were persecuted, prostitution was banned[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans1979200-387] (although [German military brothels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_military_brothels_in_World_War_II) were introduced during wartime[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008353,_354-388]), and pornography was outlawed as a moral danger.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarlston199837-389] Women wearing high heels, immodest clothes, and make-up were classified as decadent, while traditional and modest clothing was promoted.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENyrop1983126-390][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuenther2004150–151,_167–169-391] Although [nudism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nudism) was frowned upon, non-sexualized nude art—particularly in the classical [Greco-Roman style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greco-Roman_world)—was prominent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcLaughlin202286-392] After the Nazi seizure of power, births [out of wedlock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out_of_wedlock) dropped dramatically.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason1995175-393]

With Hitler's approval, Himmler intended that the new society of the Nazi regime should destigmatise illegitimate children, particularly of children fathered by members of the SS, who were vetted for racial purity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012370-394] His hope was that each SS family would have between four and six children.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012370-394] The *[Lebensborn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebensborn)* (Fountain of Life) association, founded by Himmler in 1935, created a series of maternity homes to accommodate single mothers during their pregnancies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012371-395] Both parents were examined for racial suitability before acceptance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012371-395] The resulting children were often adopted into SS families.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELongerich2012371-395] The homes were also made available to the wives of SS and Nazi Party members, who quickly filled over half the available spots.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005521-396]

Existing laws banning abortion except for medical reasons were strictly enforced by the Nazi regime, and much harsher penalties were introduced for those aborting babies, including the death sentence in 1943.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHodgson20149-397] The number of abortions declined from 35,000 per year at the start of the 1930s to fewer than 2,000 per year at the end of the decade, though in 1935 a law was passed allowing abortions for eugenics reasons.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005515-398]

### Health



Statues representing the ideal body were erected in the streets of Berlin for the [1936 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_Summer_Olympics).&#x20;

Nazi Germany had a strong [anti-tobacco movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-tobacco_movement_in_Nazi_Germany), as pioneering research by Franz H. Müller in 1939 demonstrated a causal link between smoking and lung cancer.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProctor1999196-399] The Reich Health Office took measures to try to limit smoking, including producing lectures and pamphlets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProctor1999198-400] Smoking was banned in many workplaces, on trains, and among on-duty members of the military.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProctor1999203-401] Government agencies also worked to control other carcinogenic substances such as asbestos and pesticides.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005319-402] As part of a general public health campaign, water supplies were cleaned up, lead and mercury were removed from consumer products, and women were urged to undergo regular screenings for breast cancer.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEProctor199940-403]

Government-run health care insurance plans were available, but Jews were denied coverage starting in 1933. That same year, Jewish doctors were forbidden to treat government-insured patients. In 1937, Jewish doctors were forbidden to treat non-Jewish patients, and in 1938 their right to practice medicine was removed entirely.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBusseRiesberg200420-404]

Medical experiments, many of them [pseudoscientific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscientific), were performed on concentration camp inmates beginning in 1941.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008611-405] The most notorious doctor to perform medical experiments was SS-*[Hauptsturmführer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauptsturmf%C3%BChrer)* [Josef Mengele](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Mengele), camp doctor at Auschwitz.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008608-406] Many of his victims died.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008609–661-407] Concentration camp inmates were made available for purchase by pharmaceutical companies for drug testing and other experiments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008612-408]

### Environmentalism

Further information: [Animal welfare in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_welfare_in_Nazi_Germany)

Nazi society had elements supportive of [animal rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_rights) and many people were fond of zoos and wildlife.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDeGregori2002153-409] The government took several measures to ensure the protection of animals and the environment. In 1933 the Nazis enacted a stringent animal-protection law that affected what was allowed for medical research.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHanauske-Abel199610-410] The law was only loosely enforced, and in spite of a ban on [vivisection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivisection), the Ministry of the Interior readily handed out permits for experiments on animals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUekötter200656-411]

The [Reich Forestry Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Forestry_Office) under Göring enforced regulations that required foresters to plant a variety of trees to ensure suitable habitat for wildlife, and a new Reich Animal Protection Act became law in 1933.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClosmann200530–32-412] The regime enacted the Reich Nature Protection Act in 1935 to protect the natural landscape from excessive economic development. It allowed for the expropriation of privately owned land to create nature preserves and aided in long-range planning.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClosmann200518,_30-413] Perfunctory efforts were made to curb air pollution, but little enforcement was undertaken once the war began.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUekötter2005113,_118-414]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Nazi_Germany)

When the Nazis seized power in 1933, roughly 67 per cent of the population of Germany was [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant), 33 per cent was [Roman Catholic](606848.md), while [Jews](25955086.md) made up less than 1 per cent.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005222-415][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSHMM,_''The_German_Churches_and_the_Nazi_State''-416] According to the 1939 census, taken following the annexation of Austria, 54 per cent of the population considered themselves Protestant, 40 per cent Roman Catholic, 3.5 per cent *[Gottgläubig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottgl%C3%A4ubig)* (God-believing; a Nazi religious movement) and 1.5 per cent [nonreligious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonreligious).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEricksenHeschel199910-417] Nazi Germany extensively employed Christian imagery and instituted a variety of new Christian celebrations, such as a massive celebration marking the 1200th anniversary of the birth of the Frankish emperor [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne), who [Christianised neighbouring continental Germanic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_the_Germanic_peoples) by force.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELambert2007534–538-418] Nazi propaganda stylised Hitler as a [Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christ)-like [messiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messiah), a "figure of redemption according to the Christian model", "who would liberate the world from the Antichrist".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchreiner1998345–346-419]

Under the *Gleichschaltung* process, Hitler attempted to create a [unified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_and_uniting_churches) [Protestant Reich Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reich_Church) from Germany's 28 existing Protestant [state churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landeskirche).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960237-420] The pro-Nazi [Ludwig Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ludwig_M%C3%BCller) was installed as Reich Bishop and the pro-Nazi pressure group [German Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Christians_\(movement\)) gained control of the new church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960234–238-421] They objected to the [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament) because of its Jewish origins and demanded that converted Jews be barred from their church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005220–230-422] Pastor [Martin Niemöller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Niem%C3%B6ller) responded with the formation of the [Confessing Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessing_Church), from which some clergymen opposed the Nazi regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008295–297-423] When in 1935 the Confessing Church synod protested the Nazi policy on religion, 700 of their pastors were arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerben1975140-424] Müller resigned and Hitler appointed [Hanns Kerrl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanns_Kerrl) as Minister for Church Affairs to continue efforts to control Protestantism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960238–239-425] In 1936, a Confessing Church envoy protested to Hitler against the religious persecutions and human rights abuses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerben1975140-424] Hundreds more pastors were arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960238–239-425] The church continued to resist and by early 1937 Hitler abandoned his hope of uniting the Protestant churches.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerben1975140-424] Niemöller was arrested on 1 July 1937 and spent most of the next seven years in [Sachsenhausen concentration camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sachsenhausen_concentration_camp) and Dachau.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960239-426] Theological universities were closed and pastors and theologians of other Protestant denominations were also arrested.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerben1975140-424]



Prisoner barracks at [Dachau Concentration Camp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dachau_Concentration_Camp), where the Nazis established a dedicated [clergy barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priest_Barracks_of_Dachau_Concentration_Camp) for clerical opponents of the regime in 1940

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerben1975276–277-427]

Persecution of the [Catholic Church in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Germany) followed the Nazi takeover.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008332-428] Hitler moved quickly to eliminate [political Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Catholicism), rounding up functionaries of the Catholic-aligned [Bavarian People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavarian_People's_Party) and [Catholic Centre Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Centre_Party), which along with all other non-Nazi political parties ceased to exist by July.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008290-429] The *[Reichskonkordat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskonkordat)* (Reich Concordat) treaty with the Vatican was signed in 1933, amid continuing harassment of the church in Germany.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008295-314] The treaty required the regime to honour the independence of Catholic institutions and prohibited clergy from involvement in politics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005234–235-430] Hitler routinely disregarded the Concordat, closing all Catholic institutions whose functions were not strictly religious.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGill199457-431] Clergy, nuns and lay leaders were targeted, with thousands of arrests over the ensuing years, often on trumped-up charges of currency smuggling or immorality.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960234–235-432] Several Catholic leaders were targeted in the 1934 [Night of the Long Knives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_of_the_Long_Knives) assassinations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008315-433][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConway200192-434] Most Catholic youth groups refused to dissolve themselves and Hitler Youth leader [Baldur von Schirach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldur_von_Schirach) encouraged members to attack Catholic boys in the streets.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005226,_237-435] Propaganda campaigns claimed the church was corrupt, restrictions were placed on public meetings and Catholic publications faced censorship. Catholic schools were required to reduce religious instruction and crucifixes were removed from state buildings.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005239–240-436]

[Pope Pius XI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Pius_XI) had the *"[Mit brennender Sorge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mit_brennender_Sorge)"* ("With Burning Concern") encyclical smuggled into Germany for [Passion Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passion_Sunday) 1937 and read from every pulpit as it denounced the systematic hostility of the regime toward the church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960234–235-432][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005241–243-437] In response, Goebbels renewed the regime's crackdown and propaganda against Catholics. Enrolment in denominational schools dropped sharply and by 1939 all such schools were disbanded or converted to public facilities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005245–246-438] Later Catholic protests included the 22 March 1942 pastoral letter by the German bishops on "The Struggle against Christianity and the Church".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFest1996377-439] About 30 per cent of Catholic priests were disciplined by police during the Nazi era.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005244-440][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUSHMM,_''Dachau''-441] A vast security network spied on clergy and priests were frequently denounced, arrested or sent to concentration camps – many to the dedicated [clergy barracks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priest_Barracks_of_Dachau_Concentration_Camp) at Dachau.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerben1975141–142-442] In the [areas of Poland annexed in 1939](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_areas_annexed_by_Nazi_Germany), the Nazis instigated a [brutal suppression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_persecution_of_the_Catholic_Church_in_Poland) and systematic dismantling of the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELibionka,_''The_Catholic_Church_in_Poland''-443][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavies200386,_92-444]

[Alfred Rosenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Rosenberg), head of the [Nazi Party Office of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSDAP_Office_of_Foreign_Affairs) and Hitler's appointed cultural and educational leader for Nazi Germany, considered Catholicism to be among the Nazis' chief enemies. He planned the "extermination of the foreign Christian faiths imported into Germany", and for the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bible) and [Christian cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_cross) to be replaced in all churches, cathedrals, and chapels with copies of *Mein Kampf* and the swastika. Other sects of Christianity were also targeted, with Chief of the [Nazi Party Chancellery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party_Chancellery) [Martin Bormann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Bormann) publicly proclaiming in 1941, "National Socialism and Christianity are irreconcilable."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960240-445]

## Culture

See also: [Nazi propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_propaganda)

> If the experience of the Third Reich teaches us anything, it is that a love of great music, great art and great literature does not provide people with any kind of moral or political immunization against violence, atrocity, or subservience to dictatorship.

— [Richard J. Evans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_J._Evans), *[The Coming of the Third Reich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Coming_of_the_Third_Reich)* (2003)

The regime promoted the concept of *[Volksgemeinschaft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volksgemeinschaft)*, a national German ethnic community. The goal was to build a classless society based on racial purity and the perceived need to prepare for warfare, conquest and a struggle against Marxism.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrunberger197118-446][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2008182,_203,_272-447] The German Labour Front founded the *[Kraft durch Freude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strength_Through_Joy)* (KdF; Strength Through Joy) organisation in 1933. As well as taking control of tens of thousands of privately run recreational clubs, it offered highly regimented holidays and entertainment such as cruises, vacation destinations and concerts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005465–467-448][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960265-449]

The [Reich Chamber of Culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Chamber_of_Culture) was organised under the control of the Propaganda Ministry in September 1933. Sub-chambers were set up to control aspects of cultural life such as film, radio, newspapers, fine arts, music, theatre, and literature. Members of these professions were required to join their respective organisation. Jews and people considered politically unreliable were prevented from working in the arts, and many emigrated. Books and scripts had to be approved by the Propaganda Ministry prior to publication.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960241–242-450] For example, the [Reich Chamber of Literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Chamber_of_Literature) regulated 3,000 authors, 2,500 publishers, 23,000 bookshops, and around 50 national literary prizes at the start of the war. Germany saw the publication of 20,000 new titles annually, then the highest in Europe. *[Mein Kampf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mein_Kampf)*, the bestseller by Hitler, was present in most households but was seldom actually read.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPetropoulos2018146-451] Standards deteriorated as the regime sought to use cultural outlets exclusively as propaganda.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960242-452]

Radio became popular in Germany during the 1930s; over 70 per cent of households owned a receiver by 1939, more than any other country. By July 1933, radio station staffs were purged of leftists and others deemed undesirable.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005133–135-453] Propaganda and speeches were typical radio fare immediately after the seizure of power, but as time went on Goebbels insisted more music should be played so that listeners would not turn to foreign broadcasters for entertainment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005136-454]



A Nazi book burning on 10 May 1933 in Berlin, as books by Jewish and [leftist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftist) authors are burnt

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans200516-455]

### Censorship

See also: [List of authors banned in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_authors_banned_in_Nazi_Germany)

Newspapers, like other media, were controlled by the state; the Reich Press Chamber shut down or bought newspapers and publishing houses. By 1939 over two-thirds of the newspapers and magazines were directly owned by the Propaganda Ministry.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005143–144-456] The Nazi Party daily newspaper, the *[Völkischer Beobachter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%B6lkischer_Beobachter)* ("Ethnic Observer"), was edited by Rosenberg.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960149-457] Goebbels controlled the wire services and insisted that all newspapers in Germany should only publish content favourable to the regime. Under Goebbels, the Propaganda Ministry issued two dozen directives every week on exactly what news should be published and what angles to use; the typical newspaper followed the directives closely, especially regarding what to omit.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDussel2010545,_555–557-458] Newspaper readership plummeted, partly because of the perceived decreased quality of the content and partly because of the surge in popularity of radio.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005146–147-459] Propaganda became less effective towards the end of the war, as people were able to obtain information outside official channels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDussel2010561-460]

Many authors left the country and some wrote material critical of the regime while in exile. Goebbels recommended that the remaining authors should concentrate on books themed on Germanic myths and the concept of [blood and soil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_and_soil). By the end of 1933 over a thousand books—most of them by Jewish authors or featuring Jewish characters—had been banned by the Nazi regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005152–159-461] [Nazi book burnings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_book_burnings) took place; nineteen such events were held on the night of 10 May 1933.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans200516-455] Tens of thousands of books from dozens of figures, including [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein), [Sigmund Freud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmund_Freud), [Helen Keller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helen_Keller), [Alfred Kerr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Kerr), [Marcel Proust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Proust), [Erich Maria Remarque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erich_Maria_Remarque), [Upton Sinclair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upton_Sinclair), [Jakob Wassermann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jakob_Wassermann), [H. G. Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._G._Wells), and [Émile Zola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89mile_Zola) were publicly burnt. Pacifist works, and literature espousing liberal, democratic values were targeted for destruction, as well as any writings supporting the Weimar Republic or those written by Jewish authors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960241-462]

### Architecture and art

Main articles: [Nazi architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_architecture) and [Art in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_in_Nazi_Germany)

See also: [Music in Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_in_Nazi_Germany)



Plans for Berlin called for the *[Volkshalle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkshalle)* (People's Hall) and a [triumphal arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_arch) to be built at either end of a wide boulevard.

Hitler took a personal interest in architecture and worked closely with the state architects [Paul Troost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Troost) and [Albert Speer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Speer) to create public buildings in a [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) style based on [Roman architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_architecture).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScobie199092-463][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005181-464] Speer constructed imposing structures such as the [Nazi party rally grounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_party_rally_grounds) in [Nuremberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg) and a new [Reich Chancellery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Chancellery) building in Berlin.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeer197192,_150–151-465] Hitler's plans for rebuilding Berlin included a gigantic dome based on the [Pantheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantheon,_Rome) in Rome and a [triumphal arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumphal_arch) more than double the height of the [Arc de Triomphe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arc_de_Triomphe) in Paris. Neither structure was built.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpeer1971115–116,_190-466]

Hitler's belief that [abstract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_art), [Dadaist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadaist), [expressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressionist) and [modern art](163543.md) were decadent became the basis for policy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005168-467] Many art museum directors lost their posts in 1933 and were replaced by party members.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005169-468] Some 6,500 modern works of art were removed from museums and replaced with works chosen by a Nazi jury.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer1960243–244-469] Exhibitions of the rejected pieces, under titles such as "Decadence in Art", were launched in sixteen different cities by 1935. The [Degenerate Art Exhibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Degenerate_Art_Exhibition), organised by Goebbels, ran in Munich from July to November 1937. The exhibition proved wildly popular, attracting over two million visitors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005171,_173-470]

The composer [Richard Strauss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Strauss) was appointed president of the *[Reichsmusikkammer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichsmusikkammer)* (Reich Music Chamber) on its founding in November 1933.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005187-471] As was the case with other art forms, the Nazis ostracised musicians who were deemed racially unacceptable and for the most part disapproved of music that was too modern or [atonal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atonal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005199-472] [Jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz) was considered especially inappropriate and foreign jazz musicians left the country or were expelled.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005204-473] Hitler favoured the music of [Richard Wagner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Wagner), especially pieces based on Germanic myths and heroic stories, and attended the [Bayreuth Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayreuth_Festival) each year from 1933 to 1942.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005199–200-474]

### Film

Main article: [Nazism and cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism_and_cinema)



[Leni Riefenstahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leni_Riefenstahl) (behind cameraman) at the 1936 Summer Olympics

Movies were popular in Germany in the 1930s and 1940s, with admissions of over a billion people in 1942, 1943, and 1944.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005130-475][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESPIO,_Department_of_Statistics-476] By 1934, German regulations restricting currency exports made it impossible for US filmmakers to take their profits back to America, so the major film studios closed their German branches. Exports of German films plummeted, as their antisemitic content made them impossible to show in other countries. The two largest film companies, [Universum Film AG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universum_Film_AG) and [Tobis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wien-Film), were purchased by the Propaganda Ministry, which by 1939 was producing most German films. The productions were not always overtly propagandistic, but generally had a political subtext and followed party lines regarding themes and content. Scripts were pre-censored.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005130–132-477]

[Leni Riefenstahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leni_Riefenstahl)'s *[Triumph of the Will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triumph_of_the_Will)* (1935)—documenting the 1934 Nuremberg Rally—and *[Olympia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_\(1938_film\))* (1938)—covering the [1936 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1936_Summer_Olympics)—pioneered techniques of camera movement and editing that influenced later films. New techniques such as telephoto lenses and cameras mounted on tracks were employed. Both films remain controversial, as their aesthetic merit is inseparable from their propagandising of Nazi ideals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Daily_Telegraph'',_2003-478][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2005125–126-479]

## Legacy

Main article: [Consequences of Nazism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consequences_of_Nazism)

See also: [Denazification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denazification)



Defendants in the dock at the Nuremberg trials

The Allied powers organised war crimes trials, beginning with the [Nuremberg trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuremberg_trials), held from November 1945 to October 1946, of 23 top Nazi officials. They were charged with conspiracy to commit crimes, crimes against peace, [war crimes](48626.md) and [crimes against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008741-480] All but three were found guilty and twelve were sentenced to death.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShirer19601143-481] Twelve [subsequent Nuremberg trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsequent_Nuremberg_trials) of 184 defendants were held between 1946 and 1949.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008741-480] Between 1946 and 1949, the Allies investigated 3,887 cases, of which 489 were brought to trial. The result was convictions of 1,426 people; 297 of these were sentenced to death and 279 to life in prison, with the remainder receiving lesser sentences. About 65 per cent of the death sentences were carried out.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarcuse200198-482] Poland was more active than other nations in investigating war crimes, for example prosecuting 673 of the total 789 Auschwitz staff brought to trial.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERees2005295–296-483]

The political programme espoused by Hitler and the Nazis brought about a world war, leaving behind a devastated and impoverished Europe. Germany itself suffered wholesale destruction, characterised as *[Stunde Null](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stunde_Null)* (Zero Hour).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFischer1995569-484] The number of civilians killed during the Second World War was unprecedented in the history of warfare.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurrayMillett2001554-485] As a result, Nazi ideology and the actions taken by the regime are almost universally regarded as gravely immoral.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKershaw2000a1–6-486] Historians, philosophers, and politicians often use the word "[evil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evil)" to describe Hitler and the Nazi regime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelch20012-487] Interest in Nazi Germany continues in the media and the academic world. While Evans remarks that the era "exerts an almost universal appeal because its murderous racism stands as a warning to the whole of humanity",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans200956-488] young neo-Nazis enjoy the shock value that Nazi symbols or slogans provide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Economist''2015-489] The display or use of [Nazi symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_symbolism) is illegal in Germany and Austria.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Strafgesetzbuch'',_section_86a-490]

Nazi Germany was succeeded by three states: [West Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Germany) (the Federal Republic of Germany or "FRG"), [East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) (the German Democratic Republic or "GDR"), and [Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allied-occupied_Austria).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWüstenbergArt200874–80-491] The process of denazification initiated by the Allies was only partially successful, as the need for experts in such fields as medicine and engineering was too great. However, expression of Nazi views was frowned upon, and those who expressed such views were frequently dismissed from their jobs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2008748–749-492] From the immediate post-war period through the 1950s, Germans kept quiet about their wartime experiences and felt a sense of communal guilt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESontheimer2005-493]

The trial of [Adolf Eichmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Eichmann) in 1961 and the broadcast of the television miniseries *[Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_\(miniseries\))* in 1978 brought the process of *[Vergangenheitsbewältigung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vergangenheitsbew%C3%A4ltigung)* (coping with the past) to the forefront for many Germans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Economist''2015-489][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESontheimer2005-493] Once study of Nazi Germany was introduced into the school [curriculum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curriculum) starting in the 1970s, people began researching the experiences of their family members. Study of the era and a willingness to critically examine its mistakes has led to the development of a strong democracy in Germany, but with lingering undercurrents of antisemitism and [neo-Nazi](54361.md) thought.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESontheimer2005-493]

In 2017, a [Körber Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6rber_Foundation) survey found that 47 per cent of 14- to 16-year-olds polled knew what [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp) was.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoebel2017-494][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKörber-Siftung2017-495] The journalist [Alan Posener](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Posener) attributed the country's "growing historical amnesia" in part to a failure by German film and television to reflect the country's history accurately.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPosener2018-496]

[Tümmler 2010](#CITEREFTümmler2010), p. 63.

[^cite_note-annexed-2]: &#x20;Including the [Protectorate of Bohemia and Moravia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectorate_of_Bohemia_and_Moravia) and the [General Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Government)

[^cite_note-President-3]: &#x20;as [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Germany_\(1919%E2%80%931945\))

[^cite_note-4]: &#x20;as [Führer und Reichskanzler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%BChrer_und_Reichskanzler)

[^cite_note-5]: &#x20;*De jure* from 30 April until 1 May.

[^cite_note-6]: &#x20;*De jure* from 2 May until 23 May.

[^cite_note-9]: &#x20;In 1939, before Germany acquired control of the last two regions which had been in its control before the Versailles Treaty—Alsace-Lorraine, Danzig and the Polish Corridor—its area was 633,786 km<sup>2</sup> (244,706 sq mi). See [Statistisches Jahrbuch 2006](#CITEREFStatistisches_Jahrbuch2006).

[^cite_note-15]: &#x20;[German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language): *Großdeutsches Reich* – this latter term was the official name used by the Nazis to describe their expanded political state.<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEElvert1999295_fn430-14)</sup>

[^cite_note-121]: &#x20;According to Raeder, "Our Air Force could not be counted on to guard our transports from the British Fleets, because their operations would depend on the weather, if for no other reason. It could not be expected that even for a brief period our Air Force could make up for our lack of naval supremacy." [Raeder 2001](#CITEREFRaeder2001), pp. 324–325. Grand Admiral [Karl Dönitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_D%C3%B6nitz) believed air superiority was not enough and admitted, "We possessed neither control of the air or the sea; nor were we in any position to gain it." [Dönitz 2012](#CITEREFDönitz2012), p. 114.

[^cite_note-179]: &#x20;More such districts, such as the *[Reichskommissariat Moskowien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Moskowien)* (Moscow), *[Reichskommissariat Kaukasien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Kaukasien)* (Caucasus) and *[Reichskommissariat Turkestan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reichskommissariat_Turkestan)* (Turkestan) were proposed in case these areas were brought under German rule.

[^cite_note-343]: &#x20;"Nevertheless, the available evidence suggests that, on the whole, ordinary Germans did not approve. Goebbel's propaganda campaigns carried out in the second half of 1941 and again in 1943 had failed to convert them". [Evans 2008](#CITEREFEvans2008), p. 561.
